People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess the main thing is… I feel like I’ve gotten really slowed down, especially at work. I’m a hairdresser, so I’m used to doing things with my hands pretty automatically, like sectioning hair, mixing color, moving between steps without really thinking about it. But lately it’s like my hands are moving through glue or something. Everything takes me forever now.

Even little things that should be quick, like parting someone’s hair cleanly or getting foils ready, feel like I’m dragging. And it’s not just me being picky or perfectionist—I think people are noticing. Clients, coworkers… I can tell I’m not as fast as I used to be, and it’s embarrassing because I know how to do my job. My body just won’t keep up the way it normally does.

Yeah… most days, honestly. It’s not always like crying sad, exactly, but I wake up with this heavy, flat feeling in my chest, like everything already feels pointless before I’ve even gotten out of bed. Sometimes I can still get up and do what I’m supposed to do, but underneath it there’s this sadness that feels really heavy and hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, flat feeling in your chest?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a long time now. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, that’s changed a lot. I used to be pretty… I don’t know, more alive with people, I guess. At work I used to chat with clients really easily, like ask about their kids or their trips or whatever they had going on, and it felt natural. I’d joke around, I’d remember little details, I’d help the newer stylists if they were nervous about a cut or mixing color. I actually liked being part of the energy of the salon.

Now I mostly just keep my head down and try to get through the appointment without having to give too much of myself. It’s not that I hate my clients or anything—I don’t. I just don’t have that spark in me. Even talking feels like this extra task I have to force.

And outside work, I used to go hiking with friends, even just small trails, nothing intense. I liked being outside and moving and taking pictures and stopping for coffee after. But now when people invite me, I avoid it or make excuses. It sounds exhausting before it even happens. So… yeah, the things I used to enjoy don’t really feel the same. It’s like I’m there, but I’m not really there.

Sometimes I can still get up and do what I’m supposed to do, but underneath it there’s this sadness that feels really heavy and hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, flat feeling in your chest?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a long time now. I’d say about a year and two months, roughly. It kind of settled in and just never really lifted, not in a real way. There are days where it’s a little less intense, but that heavy, flat feeling has been there for around fourteen months.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're still able to enjoy the things you used to like, or has that changed for you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s changed a lot. I used to be pretty… I don’t know, more alive with people, I guess. At work I used to chat with clients really easily, like ask about their kids or their trips or whatever they had going on, and it felt natural. I’d joke around, I’d remember little details, I’d help the newer stylists if they were nervous about a cut or mixing color. I actually liked being part of the energy of the salon.\n\nNow I mostly just keep my head down and try to get through the appointment without having to give too much of myself. It’s not that I hate my clients or anything—I don’t. I just don’t have that spark in me. Even talking feels like this extra task I have to force.\n\nAnd outside work, I used to go hiking with friends, even just small trails, nothing intense. I liked being outside and moving and taking pictures and stopping for coffee after. But now when people invite me, I avoid it or make excuses. It sounds exhausting before it even happens. So… yeah, the things I used to enjoy don’t really feel the same. It’s like I’m there, but I’m not really there.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 21 / hairdresser_hairstylist_or_cosmetologist / associates\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Negative_Symptoms\nmanifestation: \"I used to chat with clients, help the newer stylists, and go hiking with friends, but now I mostly keep my head down and avoid people because I just don’t have that spark in me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Honestly… not really much. I keep trying to think of something that I actually look forward to, and it’s like my brain just goes blank.

There are things I used to enjoy, like I said—talking with clients at the salon, getting into the rhythm of doing someone’s hair, helping the newer girls if they were unsure about something, even just being part of the noise and joking around. That used to give me something, like I felt useful or connected or… like I had some personality. Now I mostly keep my head down and do the service and try not to have to talk more than I have to. It’s not even that I’m choosing to be rude. I just feel like there’s nothing in me to pull from.

And with friends, hiking used to be something I’d look forward to. I liked the planning, the drive, being outside, stopping somewhere after. Now if someone asks me to go, I immediately start thinking of reasons I can’t. It just feels like too much effort to be around people and act like I’m enjoying it.

So… I guess maybe small things are tolerable sometimes, like being alone and not having anyone need anything from me. But actual pleasure, or looking forward to something? Not really. It feels like that part of me is kind of turned down really low.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um… I mean, small things are probably the closest I get, but I wouldn’t really call it enjoyment the way I used to. Like eating something I like or taking a warm bath might be… okay, I guess. It’s more like it’s not unpleasant, or it gives me a few minutes where nobody’s asking anything from me.

But it’s not like before, where I’d actually look forward to things. I used to get that feeling from being at the salon, talking with clients, remembering what they told me last time, helping the newer stylists if they were stuck on something. Or going hiking with friends, even just making plans and being outside. Those things used to feel like me.

Now I mostly keep my head down and avoid people when I can. I’m not trying to be cold, I just don’t have that spark in me. So small stuff can be tolerable, but pleasure feels really muted.

It feels like that part of me is kind of turned down really low.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small pleasures in life that you still enjoy, like eating your favorite food or taking a warm bath?\n\nPatient: Um… I mean, small things are probably the closest I get, but I wouldn’t really call it enjoyment the way I used to. Like eating something I like or taking a warm bath might be… okay, I guess. It’s more like it’s not unpleasant, or it gives me a few minutes where nobody’s asking anything from me.\n\nBut it’s not like before, where I’d actually look forward to things. I used to get that feeling from being at the salon, talking with clients, remembering what they told me last time, helping the newer stylists if they were stuck on something. Or going hiking with friends, even just making plans and being outside. Those things used to feel like me.\n\nNow I mostly keep my head down and avoid people when I can. I’m not trying to be cold, I just don’t have that spark in me. So small stuff can be tolerable, but pleasure feels really muted.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now find you no longer take pleasure in?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 21 / hairdresser_hairstylist_or_cosmetologist / associates\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Negative_Symptoms\nmanifestation: \"I used to chat with clients, help the newer stylists, and go hiking with friends, but now I mostly keep my head down and avoid people because I just don’t have that spark in me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, honestly, most of the things I used to enjoy feel like that now. I know I’ve kind of said it already, but it’s hard because those were the things that made me feel like myself.

At the salon, I used to be really social without even thinking about it. I’d chat with clients the whole time, ask about whatever they told me last appointment, joke around, make them feel comfortable. And I liked helping the newer stylists too, like if they were nervous about a color formula or a haircut, I’d kind of step in and talk them through it. That used to make me feel useful, like I had energy to give.

Now I mostly just keep my head down and try to get through the appointment. I don’t really want to talk unless I have to. It’s not that I suddenly don’t care about people, I just… I don’t have that spark in me anymore. Even being friendly feels like work.

And hiking with friends is another one. I used to actually look forward to it—the planning, being outside, taking pictures, stopping somewhere after. Now when someone invites me, I usually avoid it or make an excuse because the idea of being around people and pretending to enjoy myself just feels exhausting. So yeah, there are definitely things I used to love that don’t really do anything for me now.

Sometimes I can still get up and do what I’m supposed to do, but underneath it there’s this sadness that feels really heavy and hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, flat feeling in your chest?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a long time now. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do. I know I’ve kind of been circling around that, but yes, it really feels like I’ve lost interest in the things that used to make me feel alive, or at least like I was part of the world.

At work, I used to be so much more… present, I guess. I’d talk with clients the whole appointment, remember little things about their lives, joke around, help them relax. And I liked helping the newer stylists too, like if they were unsure about something, I’d step in and explain it or reassure them. That used to make me feel useful, like I had something to offer.

Now I mostly just keep my head down and try to get through the day. I avoid talking more than I have to. It’s not that I hate everyone or anything like that—I just don’t have that spark in me anymore. Even simple social stuff feels like I’m forcing it.

And with friends, hiking used to be something I actually looked forward to. Now I usually avoid it or come up with a reason not to go, because being around people and pretending to enjoy myself feels exhausting. So yeah… I’ve definitely lost interest. It’s like the activities are still there, but the part of me that cared about them isn’t really showing up.

self-reproach or guilt about being sick).\n  manifestation: I keep thinking I’m a burden on everyone and that I deserve what’s happening because I couldn’t keep my money, my job, or even basic salon responsibilities together.\n- key: S031\n  name: Diminished_Thinking\n  description: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.\n  manifestation: I stare at color formulas or the appointment screen and my brain just blanks, so I second-guess simple things I used to do automatically.\n- key: S032\n  name: Death_Suicide\n  description: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.\n  manifestation: I don’t have a plan to hurt myself, but I get thoughts like it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up and nobody had to deal with me anymore.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: psychotic_symptoms and depressive_episode occur concurrently\n  manifestation: When the sadness got heavy and stayed, the voice at the shampoo stations and the belief that someone was sabotaging my bookings were happening at the same time, not separately.\n- key: additional_requirement_2\n  name: Additional Requirement 2\n  description: depressive_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness (Criterion C)\n  manifestation: Looking back, I have spent most of this whole decline feeling depressed and weighed down, with only shorter stretches where the sadness was not as prominent.\n- key: additional_requirement_3\n  name: Additional Requirement 3\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode (Criterion B)\n  manifestation: Before the depression really took over, there was a stretch of several weeks when I was still getting up and functioning more normally, but I was already hearing the voice and feeling sure someone at the salon was interfering with my schedule.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I have lost trust from my manager and clients because I make mistakes with basic salon routines, work too slowly, and cancel things I used to reliably handle. I have also pulled away from friends and stopped showing up for volunteer haircuts at the senior center.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 8 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about a year and eight months, starting after my hours were cut and my savings began draining away.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 1 year and 4 months\n  manifestation: The voices and the feeling that someone is targeting my bookings have been happening for about a year and four months.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 1 year and 2 months\n  manifestation: The heavy depression has been with me for about a year and two months now.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 21 / hairdresser_hairstylist_or_cosmetologist / associates\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S028\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Are there any small pleasures in life that you still enjoy, like eating your favorite food or taking a warm bath?\n\nPatient: Um… I mean, small things are probably the closest I get, but I wouldn’t really call it enjoyment the way I used to. Like eating something I like or taking a warm bath might be… okay, I guess. It’s more like it’s not unpleasant, or it gives me a few minutes where nobody’s asking anything from me.\n\nBut it’s not like before, where I’d actually look forward to things. I used to get that feeling from being at the salon, talking with clients, remembering what they told me last time, helping the newer stylists if they were stuck on something. Or going hiking with friends, even just making plans and being outside. Those things used to feel like me.\n\nNow I mostly keep my head down and avoid people when I can. I’m not trying to be cold, I just don’t have that spark in me. So small stuff can be tolerable, but pleasure feels really muted.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now find you no longer take pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, honestly, most of the things I used to enjoy feel like that now. I know I’ve kind of said it already, but it’s hard because those were the things that made me feel like myself.\n\nAt the salon, I used to be really social without even thinking about it. I’d chat with clients the whole time, ask about whatever they told me last appointment, joke around, make them feel comfortable. And I liked helping the newer stylists too, like if they were nervous about a color formula or a haircut, I’d kind of step in and talk them through it. That used to make me feel useful, like I had energy to give.\n\nNow I mostly just keep my head down and try to get through the appointment. I don’t really want to talk unless I have to. It’s not that I suddenly don’t care about people, I just… I don’t have that spark in me anymore. Even being friendly feels like work.\n\nAnd hiking with friends is another one. I used to actually look forward to it—the planning, being outside, taking pictures, stopping somewhere after. Now when someone invites me, I usually avoid it or make an excuse because the idea of being around people and pretending to enjoy myself just feels exhausting. So yeah, there are definitely things I used to love that don’t really do anything for me now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I know I’ve kind of been circling around that, but yes, it really feels like I’ve lost interest in the things that used to make me feel alive, or at least like I was part of the world.\n\nAt work, I used to be so much more… present, I guess. I’d talk with clients the whole appointment, remember little things about their lives, joke around, help them relax. And I liked helping the newer stylists too, like if they were unsure about something, I’d step in and explain it or reassure them. That used to make me feel useful, like I had something to offer.\n\nNow I mostly just keep my head down and try to get through the day. I avoid talking more than I have to. It’s not that I hate everyone or anything like that—I just don’t have that spark in me anymore. Even simple social stuff feels like I’m forcing it.\n\nAnd with friends, hiking used to be something I actually looked forward to. Now I usually avoid it or come up with a reason not to go, because being around people and pretending to enjoy myself feels exhausting. So yeah… I’ve definitely lost interest. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. I haven’t been weighing myself every day or anything, so I don’t have an exact number, but my clothes have definitely been looser. Like jeans that used to fit pretty snug are kind of slipping more, and I noticed it with a couple shirts too.

I think it’s probably because I keep skipping meals without really planning to. It’s not even like I’m trying to diet or anything. I’ll just have coffee in the morning, then get distracted or just not feel like making food, and then by nighttime I realize I’ve only had coffee and maybe a few bites of something. Then I feel kind of stupid for not noticing sooner, but it keeps happening.

So… yeah, I’d say my weight has probably gone down some, even if I don’t know exactly how much.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main thing is… I feel like I’ve gotten really slowed down, especially at work. I’m a hairdresser, so I’m used to doing things with my hands pretty automatically, like sectioning hair, mixing color, moving between steps without really thinking about it. But lately it’s like my hands are moving through glue or something. Everything takes me forever now.\n\nEven little things that should be quick, like parting someone’s hair cleanly or getting foils ready, feel like I’m dragging. And it’s not just me being picky or perfectionist—I think people are noticing. Clients, coworkers… I can tell I’m not as fast as I used to be, and it’s embarrassing because I know how to do my job. My body just won’t keep up the way it normally does.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, honestly. It’s not always like crying sad, exactly, but I wake up with this heavy, flat feeling in my chest, like everything already feels pointless before I’ve even gotten out of bed. Like I had this idea of the life I was trying to build—work, being good at what I do, having things feel like they’re moving forward—and lately it just feels like… what’s the point of even trying if I’m going to feel this way through all of it?\n\nIt’s there most days. Sometimes I can still get up and do what I’m supposed to do, but underneath it there’s this sadness that feels really heavy and hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, flat feeling in your chest?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a long time now. I’d say about a year and two months, roughly. It kind of settled in and just never really lifted, not in a real way. There are days where it’s a little less intense, but that heavy, flat feeling has been there for around fourteen months.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're still able to enjoy the things you used to like, or has that changed for you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s changed a lot. I used to be pretty… I don’t know, more alive with people, I guess. At work I used to chat with clients really easily, like ask about their kids or their trips or whatever they had going on, and it felt natural. I’d joke around, I’d remember little details, I’d help the newer stylists if they were nervous about a cut or mixing color. I actually liked being part of the energy of the salon.\n\nNow I mostly just keep my head down and try to get through the appointment without having to give too much of myself. It’s not that I hate my clients or anything—I don’t. I just don’t have that spark in me. Even talking feels like this extra task I have to force.\n\nAnd outside work, I used to go hiking with friends, even just small trails, nothing intense. I liked being outside and moving and taking pictures and stopping for coffee after. But now when people invite me, I avoid it or make excuses. It sounds exhausting before it even happens. So… yeah, the things I used to enjoy don’t really feel the same. It’s like I’m there, but I’m not really there.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not really much. I keep trying to think of something that I actually look forward to, and it’s like my brain just goes blank.\n\nThere are things I used to enjoy, like I said—talking with clients at the salon, getting into the rhythm of doing someone’s hair, helping the newer girls if they were unsure about something, even just being part of the noise and joking around. That used to give me something, like I felt useful or connected or… like I had some personality. Now I mostly keep my head down and do the service and try not to have to talk more than I have to. It’s not even that I’m choosing to be rude. I just feel like there’s nothing in me to pull from.\n\nAnd with friends, hiking used to be something I’d look forward to. I liked the planning, the drive, being outside, stopping somewhere after. Now if someone asks me to go, I immediately start thinking of reasons I can’t. It just feels like too much effort to be around people and act like I’m enjoying it.\n\nSo… I guess maybe small things are tolerable sometimes, like being alone and not having anyone need anything from me. But actual pleasure, or looking forward to something? Not really. It feels like that part of me is kind of turned down really low.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small pleasures in life that you still enjoy, like eating your favorite food or taking a warm bath?\n\nPatient: Um… I mean, small things are probably the closest I get, but I wouldn’t really call it enjoyment the way I used to. It feels like that part of me is kind of turned down really low.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small pleasures in life that you still enjoy, like eating your favorite food or taking a warm bath?\n\nPatient: Um… I mean, small things are probably the closest I get, but I wouldn’t really call it enjoyment the way I used to. Like eating something I like or taking a warm bath might be… okay, I guess. It’s more like it’s not unpleasant, or it gives me a few minutes where nobody’s asking anything from me.\n\nBut it’s not like before, where I’d actually look forward to things. I used to get that feeling from being at the salon, talking with clients, remembering what they told me last time, helping the newer stylists if they were stuck on something. Or going hiking with friends, even just making plans and being outside. Those things used to feel like me.\n\nNow I mostly keep my head down and avoid people when I can. I’m not trying to be cold, I just don’t have that spark in me. So small stuff can be tolerable, but pleasure feels really muted.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now find you no longer take pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, honestly, most of the things I used to enjoy feel like that now. I know I’ve kind of said it already, but it’s hard because those were the things that made me feel like myself.\n\nAt the salon, I used to be really social without even thinking about it. I’d chat with clients the whole time, ask about whatever they told me last appointment, joke around, make them feel comfortable. And I liked helping the newer stylists too, like if they were nervous about a color formula or a haircut, I’d kind of step in and talk them through it. That used to make me feel useful, like I had energy to give.\n\nNow I mostly just keep my head down and try to get through the appointment. I don’t really want to talk unless I have to. It’s not that I suddenly don’t care about people, I just… I don’t have that spark in me anymore. Even being friendly feels like work.\n\nAnd hiking with friends is another one. I used to actually look forward to it—the planning, being outside, taking pictures, stopping somewhere after. Now when someone invites me, I usually avoid it or make an excuse because the idea of being around people and pretending to enjoy myself just feels exhausting. So yeah, there are definitely things I used to love that don’t really do anything for me now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I know I’ve kind of been circling around that, but yes, it really feels like I’ve lost interest in the things that used to make me feel alive, or at least like I was part of the world.\n\nAt work, I used to be so much more… present, I guess. I’d talk with clients the whole appointment, remember little things about their lives, joke around, help them relax. And I liked helping the newer stylists too, like if they were unsure about something, I’d step in and explain it or reassure them. That used to make me feel useful, like I had something to offer.\n\nNow I mostly just keep my head down and try to get through the day. I avoid talking more than I have to. It’s not that I hate everyone or anything like that—I just don’t have that spark in me anymore. Even simple social stuff feels like I’m forcing it.\n\nAnd with friends, hiking used to be something I actually looked forward to. It’s like the activities are still there, but the part of me that cared about them isn’t really showing up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I haven’t been weighing myself every day or anything, so I don’t have an exact number, but my clothes have definitely been looser. Like jeans that used to fit pretty snug are kind of slipping more, and I noticed it with a couple shirts too.\n\nI think it’s probably because I keep skipping meals without really planning to. It’s not even like I’m trying to diet or anything. I’ll just have coffee in the morning, then get distracted or just not feel like making food, and then by nighttime I realize I’ve only had coffee and maybe a few bites of something. Sometimes I can still get up and do what I’m supposed to do, but underneath it there’s this sadness that feels really heavy and hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, flat feeling in your chest?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a long time now. I’d say about a year and two months, roughly. It kind of settled in and just never really lifted, not in a real way. I’d chat with clients the whole time, ask about whatever they told me last appointment, joke around, make them feel comfortable. That used to make me feel useful, like I had energy to give.\n\nNow I mostly just keep my head down and try to get through the appointment. I don’t really want to talk unless I have to. I used to actually look forward to it—the planning, being outside, taking pictures, stopping somewhere after. Now when someone invites me, I usually avoid it or make an excuse because the idea of being around people and pretending to enjoy myself just feels exhausting. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… most of the time, I think so. I don’t really like admitting that, because I used to be someone people could count on, especially at the salon. But lately it’s been affecting almost everything I’m supposed to do.

Like at work, I’m making mistakes with really basic routines that I’ve done a million times before. Nothing that should be hard, but I’ll miss steps or move so slowly that appointments run behind, and then I can tell my manager is frustrated. I feel like she doesn’t trust me the same way anymore. And some clients notice too, or they seem less comfortable booking with me, which feels awful because that relationship with clients used to be something I was good at.

I’ve also been canceling things I used to handle without even thinking. Like tasks at work or plans I agreed to, and then I just can’t make myself follow through. It’s embarrassing because it makes me seem flaky, but it’s not like I suddenly don’t care. I just feel like I can’t keep up.

And outside of work, I’ve pulled away from friends a lot. I don’t show up the way I used to. I also stopped going to do volunteer haircuts at the senior center, and that used to be important to me. I used to feel good doing that, like I was actually helping people, but now I just… don’t go.

So yeah, it’s not just a bad mood in the background. It’s interfering with my work, my relationships, and the things I used to be responsible for most days.

routines, work too slowly, and cancel things I used to reliably handle. I have also pulled away from friends and stopped showing up for volunteer haircuts at the senior center.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 8 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about a year and eight months, starting after my hours were cut and my savings began draining away.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 1 year and 4 months\n  manifestation: The voices and the feeling that someone is targeting my bookings have been happening for about a year and four months.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 1 year and 2 months\n  manifestation: The heavy depression has been with me for about a year and two months now.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 21 / hairdresser_hairstylist_or_cosmetologist / associates\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S028\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I haven’t been weighing myself every day or anything, so I don’t have an exact number, but my clothes have definitely been looser. Like jeans that used to fit pretty snug are kind of slipping more, and I noticed it with a couple shirts too.\n\nI think it’s probably because I keep skipping meals without really planning to. It’s not even like I’m trying to diet or anything. I’ll just have coffee in the morning, then get distracted or just not feel like making food, and then by nighttime I realize I’ve only had coffee and maybe a few bites of something. Then I feel kind of stupid for not noticing sooner, but it keeps happening.\n\nSo… yeah, I’d say my weight has probably gone down some, even if I don’t know exactly how much.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your weight loss has been more than 5% of your body weight over the past year?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure if it’s more than 5%, because I honestly haven’t been tracking it that closely. I know that’s probably the kind of thing I should know, but I’ve just been noticing it more from my clothes than from the scale.\n\nLike, my jeans feel looser, and some shirts don’t fit the same. And it lines up with how I’ve been eating, because I keep skipping meals without really meaning to. I’ll have coffee, then I get through the day and realize at night that I only had coffee and maybe a few bites of something. So I do think I’ve lost weight, I just can’t say for sure if it’s over 5%.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your depressive symptoms are interfering with your daily life most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most of the time, I think so. I don’t really like admitting that, because I used to be someone people could count on, especially at the salon. But lately it’s been affecting almost everything I’m supposed to do.\n\nLike at work, I’m making mistakes with really basic routines that I’ve done a million times before. Nothing that should be hard, but I’ll miss steps or move so slowly that appointments run behind, and then I can tell my manager is frustrated. I feel like she doesn’t trust me the same way anymore. And some clients notice too, or they seem less comfortable booking with me, which feels awful because that relationship with clients used to be something I was good at.\n\nI’ve also been canceling things I used to handle without even thinking. Like tasks at work or plans I agreed to, and then I just can’t make myself follow through. It’s embarrassing because it makes me seem flaky, but it’s not like I suddenly don’t care. I just feel like I can’t keep up.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve pulled away from friends a lot. I don’t show up the way I used to. I also stopped going to do volunteer haircuts at the senior center, and that used to be important to me. I used to feel good doing that, like I was actually helping people, but now I just… don’t go.\n\nSo yeah, it’s not just a bad mood in the background. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think that’s a good way to put it. It’s like things that used to make me feel happy or connected just don’t really land the same anymore.

At the salon, I used to be really chatty with clients. Not in a fake way, like I actually liked hearing about their lives and joking around and making them feel comfortable. I’d remember little details, like whose daughter was graduating or who was going on a trip, and I liked that part of the job almost as much as doing the hair. And I used to help the newer stylists too, like if someone was nervous about a color formula or just needed reassurance, I’d jump in. That made me feel useful.

But now I mostly just keep my head down and try to get through the appointment. I’m not trying to be rude, but talking feels like this huge effort, like I don’t have the energy or the spark in me. Even when a client is being nice, I don’t really feel that warm feeling I used to get.

And with friends, same thing. I used to go hiking with them and actually enjoy being outside and laughing and taking pictures and all that. Now even if they invite me, I mostly avoid it. It just sounds exhausting, and I don’t feel excited about it the way I should. It’s not that I hate everyone or anything… it’s more like the part of me that used to enjoy people and doing things is kind of muted.

But lately it’s like my hands are moving through glue or something. And it’s not just me being picky or perfectionist—I think people are noticing. Clients, coworkers… I can tell I’m not as fast as I used to be, and it’s embarrassing because I know how to do my job. My body just won’t keep up the way it normally does.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, honestly. It’s not always like crying sad, exactly, but I wake up with this heavy, flat feeling in my chest, like everything already feels pointless before I’ve even gotten out of bed. Like I had this idea of the life I was trying to build—work, being good at what I do, having things feel like they’re moving forward—and lately it just feels like… what’s the point of even trying if I’m going to feel this way through all of it?\n\nIt’s there most days. Sometimes I can still get up and do what I’m supposed to do, but underneath it there’s this sadness that feels really heavy and hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, flat feeling in your chest?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a long time now. I’d say about a year and two months, roughly. It kind of settled in and just never really lifted, not in a real way. There are days where it’s a little less intense, but that heavy, flat feeling has been there for around fourteen months.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're still able to enjoy the things you used to like, or has that changed for you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s changed a lot. I used to be pretty… I don’t know, more alive with people, I guess. At work I used to chat with clients really easily, like ask about their kids or their trips or whatever they had going on, and it felt natural. I’d joke around, I’d remember little details, I’d help the newer stylists if they were nervous about a cut or mixing color. I actually liked being part of the energy of the salon.\n\nNow I mostly just keep my head down and try to get through the appointment without having to give too much of myself. It’s not that I hate my clients or anything—I don’t. I just don’t have that spark in me. Even talking feels like this extra task I have to force.\n\nAnd outside work, I used to go hiking with friends, even just small trails, nothing intense. I liked being outside and moving and taking pictures and stopping for coffee after. But now when people invite me, I avoid it or make excuses. It sounds exhausting before it even happens. So… yeah, the things I used to enjoy don’t really feel the same. It’s like I’m there, but I’m not really there.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not really much. That used to give me something, like I felt useful or connected or… like I had some personality. Now I mostly keep my head down and do the service and try not to have to talk more than I have to. It’s not even that I’m choosing to be rude. I just feel like there’s nothing in me to pull from.\n\nAnd with friends, hiking used to be something I’d look forward to. I liked the planning, the drive, being outside, stopping somewhere after. Now if someone asks me to go, I immediately start thinking of reasons I can’t. It just feels like too much effort to be around people and act like I’m enjoying it.\n\nSo… I guess maybe small things are tolerable sometimes, like being alone and not having anyone need anything from me. Like eating something I like or taking a warm bath might be… okay, I guess. I used to get that feeling from being at the salon, talking with clients, remembering what they told me last time, helping the newer stylists if they were stuck on something. It’s like I’m there, but I’m not really there.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not really much. I keep trying to think of something that I actually look forward to, and it’s like my brain just goes blank.\n\nThere are things I used to enjoy, like I said—talking with clients at the salon, getting into the rhythm of doing someone’s hair, helping the newer girls if they were unsure about something, even just being part of the noise and joking around. That used to give me something, like I felt useful or connected or… like I had some personality. Now I mostly keep my head down and do the service and try not to have to talk more than I have to. It’s not even that I’m choosing to be rude. I just feel like there’s nothing in me to pull from.\n\nAnd with friends, hiking used to be something I’d look forward to. I liked the planning, the drive, being outside, stopping somewhere after. Now if someone asks me to go, I immediately start thinking of reasons I can’t. It just feels like too much effort to be around people and act like I’m enjoying it.\n\nSo… I guess maybe small things are tolerable sometimes, like being alone and not having anyone need anything from me. But actual pleasure, or looking forward to something? Not really. It feels like that part of me is kind of turned down really low.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small pleasures in life that you still enjoy, like eating your favorite food or taking a warm bath?\n\nPatient: Um… I mean, small things are probably the closest I get, but I wouldn’t really call it enjoyment the way I used to. Like eating something I like or taking a warm bath might be… okay, I guess. It’s more like it’s not unpleasant, or it gives me a few minutes where nobody’s asking anything from me.\n\nBut it’s not like before, where I’d actually look forward to things. I used to get that feeling from being at the salon, talking with clients, remembering what they told me last time, helping the newer stylists if they were stuck on something. Or going hiking with friends, even just making plans and being outside. Those things used to feel like me.\n\nNow I mostly keep my head down and avoid people when I can. I’m not trying to be cold, I just don’t have that spark in me. So small stuff can be tolerable, but pleasure feels really muted.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now find you no longer take pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, honestly, most of the things I used to enjoy feel like that now. I know I’ve kind of said it already, but it’s hard because those were the things that made me feel like myself.\n\nAt the salon, I used to be really social without even thinking about it. I’d chat with clients the whole time, ask about whatever they told me last appointment, joke around, make them feel comfortable. And I liked helping the newer stylists too, like if they were nervous about a color formula or a haircut, I’d kind of step in and talk them through it. That used to make me feel useful, like I had energy to give.\n\nNow I mostly just keep my head down and try to get through the appointment. I don’t really want to talk unless I have to. It’s not that I suddenly don’t care about people, I just… I don’t have that spark in me anymore. Even being friendly feels like work.\n\nAnd hiking with friends is another one. I used to actually look forward to it—the planning, being outside, taking pictures, stopping somewhere after. Now when someone invites me, I usually avoid it or make an excuse because the idea of being around people and pretending to enjoy myself just feels exhausting. So yeah, there are definitely things I used to love that don’t really do anything for me now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I know I’ve kind of been circling around that, but yes, it really feels like I’ve lost interest in the things that used to make me feel alive, or at least like I was part of the world.\n\nAt work, I used to be so much more… present, I guess. I’d talk with clients the whole appointment, remember little things about their lives, joke around, help them relax. And I liked helping the newer stylists too, like if they were unsure about something, I’d step in and explain it or reassure them. That used to make me feel useful, like I had something to offer.\n\nNow I mostly just keep my head down and try to get through the day. I avoid talking more than I have to. It’s not that I hate everyone or anything like that—I just don’t have that spark in me anymore. Even simple social stuff feels like I’m forcing it.\n\nAnd with friends, hiking used to be something I actually looked forward to. Now I usually avoid it or come up with a reason not to go, because being around people and pretending to enjoy myself feels exhausting. So yeah… I’ve definitely lost interest. It’s like the activities are still there, but the part of me that cared about them isn’t really showing up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I haven’t been weighing myself every day or anything, so I don’t have an exact number, but my clothes have definitely been looser. Like jeans that used to fit pretty snug are kind of slipping more, and I noticed it with a couple shirts too.\n\nI think it’s probably because I keep skipping meals without really planning to. It’s not even like I’m trying to diet or anything. I’ll just have coffee in the morning, then get distracted or just not feel like making food, and then by nighttime I realize I’ve only had coffee and maybe a few bites of something. Then I feel kind of stupid for not noticing sooner, but it keeps happening.\n\nSo… yeah, I’d say my weight has probably gone down some, even if I don’t know exactly how much.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your weight loss has been more than 5% of your body weight over the past year?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure if it’s more than 5%, because I honestly haven’t been tracking it that closely. I know that’s probably the kind of thing I should know, but I’ve just been noticing it more from my clothes than from the scale.\n\nLike, my jeans feel looser, and some shirts don’t fit the same. And it lines up with how I’ve been eating, because I keep skipping meals without really meaning to. I’ll have coffee, then I get through the day and realize at night that I only had coffee and maybe a few bites of something. So I do think I’ve lost weight, I just can’t say for sure if it’s over 5%.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your depressive symptoms are interfering with your daily life most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most of the time, I think so. I don’t really like admitting that, because I used to be someone people could count on, especially at the salon. But lately it’s been affecting almost everything I’m supposed to do.\n\nLike at work, I’m making mistakes with really basic routines that I’ve done a million times before. Nothing that should be hard, but I’ll miss steps or move so slowly that appointments run behind, and then I can tell my manager is frustrated. I feel like she doesn’t trust me the same way anymore. And some clients notice too, or they seem less comfortable booking with me, which feels awful because that relationship with clients used to be something I was good at.\n\nI’ve also been canceling things I used to handle without even thinking. Like tasks at work or plans I agreed to, and then I just can’t make myself follow through. It’s embarrassing because it makes me seem flaky, but it’s not like I suddenly don’t care. I just feel like I can’t keep up.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve pulled away from friends a lot. I don’t show up the way I used to. I also stopped going to do volunteer haircuts at the senior center, and that used to be important to me. I used to feel good doing that, like I was actually helping people, but now I just… don’t go.\n\nSo yeah, it’s not just a bad mood in the background. It’s interfering with my work, my relationships, and the things I used to be responsible for most days.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that would normally bring you happiness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s a good way to put it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d call it “severe” all the time, because some days I can still show up and sort of get through things, but… it feels serious, if that makes sense. Like it’s not just me being a little down or having a bad week.

The biggest thing is how much it’s changed how I function. At the salon, I used to be pretty reliable. My manager could count on me, clients trusted me, and I knew my routines. Now I’m making mistakes with basic stuff I’ve done over and over, or I’m working so slowly that everything gets backed up. And I can tell people notice. My manager seems like she doesn’t trust me the same way, and some clients don’t seem as comfortable with me, which really hurts because that used to be the part of my life where I felt competent.

I’ve also been canceling things I used to handle without making a big deal out of it. Like responsibilities at work, or things I said I’d do, and then I just don’t follow through. It makes me feel ashamed because I know it looks like I don’t care, but it’s more like I can’t get myself together enough.

And outside of work, I’ve pulled away from my friends a lot. I don’t show up like I used to. I also stopped doing the volunteer haircuts at the senior center, which I used to be really consistent with. That was something I actually cared about, and now I just… don’t go.

So I guess I’d say moderate to maybe really disruptive, most of the time. Maybe not the worst it could possibly be, but definitely enough that it’s messing up my work, my relationships, and the things I used to be dependable for.

Everything takes me forever now.\n\nEven little things that should be quick, like parting someone’s hair cleanly or getting foils ready, feel like I’m dragging. And it’s not just me being picky or perfectionist—I think people are noticing. Clients, coworkers… I can tell I’m not as fast as I used to be, and it’s embarrassing because I know how to do my job. My body just won’t keep up the way it normally does.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, honestly. It’s not always like crying sad, exactly, but I wake up with this heavy, flat feeling in my chest, like everything already feels pointless before I’ve even gotten out of bed. Like I had this idea of the life I was trying to build—work, being good at what I do, having things feel like they’re moving forward—and lately it just feels like… what’s the point of even trying if I’m going to feel this way through all of it?\n\nIt’s there most days. Sometimes I can still get up and do what I’m supposed to do, but underneath it there’s this sadness that feels really heavy and hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, flat feeling in your chest?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a long time now. I’d say about a year and two months, roughly. It kind of settled in and just never really lifted, not in a real way. There are days where it’s a little less intense, but that heavy, flat feeling has been there for around fourteen months.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're still able to enjoy the things you used to like, or has that changed for you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s changed a lot. I used to be pretty… I don’t know, more alive with people, I guess. At work I used to chat with clients really easily, like ask about their kids or their trips or whatever they had going on, and it felt natural. I’d joke around, I’d remember little details, I’d help the newer stylists if they were nervous about a cut or mixing color. I actually liked being part of the energy of the salon.\n\nNow I mostly just keep my head down and try to get through the appointment without having to give too much of myself. It’s not that I hate my clients or anything—I don’t. I just don’t have that spark in me. Even talking feels like this extra task I have to force.\n\nAnd outside work, I used to go hiking with friends, even just small trails, nothing intense. I liked being outside and moving and taking pictures and stopping for coffee after. But now when people invite me, I avoid it or make excuses. It sounds exhausting before it even happens. So… yeah, the things I used to enjoy don’t really feel the same. It’s like I’m there, but I’m not really there.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not really much. I keep trying to think of something that I actually look forward to, and it’s like my brain just goes blank.\n\nThere are things I used to enjoy, like I said—talking with clients at the salon, getting into the rhythm of doing someone’s hair, helping the newer girls if they were unsure about something, even just being part of the noise and joking around. That used to give me something, like I felt useful or connected or… like I had some personality. Now I mostly keep my head down and do the service and try not to have to talk more than I have to. It’s not even that I’m choosing to be rude. I just feel like there’s nothing in me to pull from.\n\nAnd with friends, hiking used to be something I’d look forward to. I liked the planning, the drive, being outside, stopping somewhere after. Now if someone asks me to go, I immediately start thinking of reasons I can’t. It just feels like too much effort to be around people and act like I’m enjoying it.\n\nSo… I guess maybe small things are tolerable sometimes, like being alone and not having anyone need anything from me. But actual pleasure, or looking forward to something? Not really. It feels like that part of me is kind of turned down really low.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small pleasures in life that you still enjoy, like eating your favorite food or taking a warm bath?\n\nPatient: Um… I mean, small things are probably the closest I get, but I wouldn’t really call it enjoyment the way I used to. Like eating something I like or taking a warm bath might be… okay, I guess. It’s more like it’s not unpleasant, or it gives me a few minutes where nobody’s asking anything from me.\n\nBut it’s not like before, where I’d actually look forward to things. I used to get that feeling from being at the salon, talking with clients, remembering what they told me last time, helping the newer stylists if they were stuck on something. Or going hiking with friends, even just making plans and being outside. Those things used to feel like me.\n\nNow I mostly keep my head down and avoid people when I can. I’m not trying to be cold, I just don’t have that spark in me. So small stuff can be tolerable, but pleasure feels really muted.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy, but now find you no longer take pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, honestly, most of the things I used to enjoy feel like that now. I know I’ve kind of said it already, but it’s hard because those were the things that made me feel like myself.\n\nAt the salon, I used to be really social without even thinking about it. I’d chat with clients the whole time, ask about whatever they told me last appointment, joke around, make them feel comfortable. And I liked helping the newer stylists too, like if they were nervous about a color formula or a haircut, I’d kind of step in and talk them through it. That used to make me feel useful, like I had energy to give.\n\nNow I mostly just keep my head down and try to get through the appointment. I don’t really want to talk unless I have to. It’s not that I suddenly don’t care about people, I just… I don’t have that spark in me anymore. Even being friendly feels like work.\n\nAnd hiking with friends is another one. I used to actually look forward to it—the planning, being outside, taking pictures, stopping somewhere after. Now when someone invites me, I usually avoid it or make an excuse because the idea of being around people and pretending to enjoy myself just feels exhausting. So yeah, there are definitely things I used to love that don’t really do anything for me now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I know I’ve kind of been circling around that, but yes, it really feels like I’ve lost interest in the things that used to make me feel alive, or at least like I was part of the world.\n\nAt work, I used to be so much more… present, I guess. I’d talk with clients the whole appointment, remember little things about their lives, joke around, help them relax. And I liked helping the newer stylists too, like if they were unsure about something, I’d step in and explain it or reassure them. That used to make me feel useful, like I had something to offer.\n\nNow I mostly just keep my head down and try to get through the day. I avoid talking more than I have to. It’s not that I hate everyone or anything like that—I just don’t have that spark in me anymore. Even simple social stuff feels like I’m forcing it.\n\nAnd with friends, hiking used to be something I actually looked forward to. Now I usually avoid it or come up with a reason not to go, because being around people and pretending to enjoy myself feels exhausting. So yeah… I’ve definitely lost interest. It’s like the activities are still there, but the part of me that cared about them isn’t really showing up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I haven’t been weighing myself every day or anything, so I don’t have an exact number, but my clothes have definitely been looser. Like jeans that used to fit pretty snug are kind of slipping more, and I noticed it with a couple shirts too.\n\nI think it’s probably because I keep skipping meals without really planning to. It’s not even like I’m trying to diet or anything. I’ll just have coffee in the morning, then get distracted or just not feel like making food, and then by nighttime I realize I’ve only had coffee and maybe a few bites of something. Then I feel kind of stupid for not noticing sooner, but it keeps happening.\n\nSo… yeah, I’d say my weight has probably gone down some, even if I don’t know exactly how much.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your weight loss has been more than 5% of your body weight over the past year?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure if it’s more than 5%, because I honestly haven’t been tracking it that closely. I know that’s probably the kind of thing I should know, but I’ve just been noticing it more from my clothes than from the scale.\n\nLike, my jeans feel looser, and some shirts don’t fit the same. And it lines up with how I’ve been eating, because I keep skipping meals without really meaning to. I’ll have coffee, then I get through the day and realize at night that I only had coffee and maybe a few bites of something. So I do think I’ve lost weight, I just can’t say for sure if it’s over 5%.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your depressive symptoms are interfering with your daily life most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most of the time, I think so. I don’t really like admitting that, because I used to be someone people could count on, especially at the salon. But lately it’s been affecting almost everything I’m supposed to do.\n\nLike at work, I’m making mistakes with really basic routines that I’ve done a million times before. Nothing that should be hard, but I’ll miss steps or move so slowly that appointments run behind, and then I can tell my manager is frustrated. I feel like she doesn’t trust me the same way anymore. And some clients notice too, or they seem less comfortable booking with me, which feels awful because that relationship with clients used to be something I was good at.\n\nI’ve also been canceling things I used to handle without even thinking. Like tasks at work or plans I agreed to, and then I just can’t make myself follow through. It’s embarrassing because it makes me seem flaky, but it’s not like I suddenly don’t care. I just feel like I can’t keep up.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve pulled away from friends a lot. I don’t show up the way I used to. I also stopped going to do volunteer haircuts at the senior center, and that used to be important to me. I used to feel good doing that, like I was actually helping people, but now I just… don’t go.\n\nSo yeah, it’s not just a bad mood in the background. It’s interfering with my work, my relationships, and the things I used to be responsible for most days.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that would normally bring you happiness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s a good way to put it. It’s like things that used to make me feel happy or connected just don’t really land the same anymore.\n\nAt the salon, I used to be really chatty with clients. Not in a fake way, like I actually liked hearing about their lives and joking around and making them feel comfortable. I’d remember little details, like whose daughter was graduating or who was going on a trip, and I liked that part of the job almost as much as doing the hair. And I used to help the newer stylists too, like if someone was nervous about a color formula or just needed reassurance, I’d jump in. That made me feel useful.\n\nBut now I mostly just keep my head down and try to get through the appointment. I’m not trying to be rude, but talking feels like this huge effort, like I don’t have the energy or the spark in me. Even when a client is being nice, I don’t really feel that warm feeling I used to get.\n\nAnd with friends, same thing. I used to go hiking with them and actually enjoy being outside and laughing and taking pictures and all that. Now even if they invite me, I mostly avoid it. It just sounds exhausting, and I don’t feel excited about it the way I should. It’s not that I hate everyone or anything… it’s more like the part of me that used to enjoy people and doing things is kind of muted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your depressive symptoms are severe most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “severe” all the time, because some days I can still show up and sort of get through things, but… it feels serious, if that makes sense. Like it’s not just me being a little down or having a bad week.\n\nThe biggest thing is how much it’s changed how I function. At the salon, I used to be pretty reliable. My manager could count on me, clients trusted me, and I knew my routines. Now I’m making mistakes with basic stuff I’ve done over and over, or I’m working so slowly that everything gets backed up. And I can tell people notice. My manager seems like she doesn’t trust me the same way, and some clients don’t seem as comfortable with me, which really hurts because that used to be the part of my life where I felt competent.\n\nI’ve also been canceling things I used to handle without making a big deal out of it. Like responsibilities at work, or things I said I’d do, and then I just don’t follow through. It makes me feel ashamed because I know it looks like I don’t care, but it’s more like I can’t get myself together enough.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve pulled away from my friends a lot. I don’t show up like I used to. I also stopped doing the volunteer haircuts at the senior center, which I used to be really consistent with. That was something I actually cared about, and now I just… don’t go.\n\nSo I guess I’d say moderate to maybe really disruptive, most of the time. Maybe not the worst it could possibly be, but definitely enough that it’s messing up my work, my relationships, and the things I used to be dependable for.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um… yeah, kind of. I don’t have, like, a plan to hurt myself or anything like that. I’m not sitting there thinking of a specific way to do it. But I do get these thoughts that scare me a little, or at least make me feel ashamed to say out loud.

It’s more like… I’ll think it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Like if I could just not have to deal with everything, and nobody else had to deal with me either. Not exactly that I’m trying to do something to myself, but more that disappearing or not existing for a while feels like it would be a relief.

And then I feel guilty because I know that sounds awful, and I don’t want people to think I’m being dramatic. It’s just when I’m really low, my mind goes there, like everyone would be less burdened if I wasn’t around. But no, I don’t have a plan.

I used to feel good doing that, like I was actually helping people, but now I just… don’t go.\n\nSo yeah, it’s not just a bad mood in the background. It’s interfering with my work, my relationships, and the things I used to be responsible for most days.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that would normally bring you happiness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s a good way to put it. It’s like things that used to make me feel happy or connected just don’t really land the same anymore.\n\nAt the salon, I used to be really chatty with clients. Not in a fake way, like I actually liked hearing about their lives and joking around and making them feel comfortable. I’d remember little details, like whose daughter was graduating or who was going on a trip, and I liked that part of the job almost as much as doing the hair. And I used to help the newer stylists too, like if someone was nervous about a color formula or just needed reassurance, I’d jump in. That made me feel useful.\n\nBut now I mostly just keep my head down and try to get through the appointment. I’m not trying to be rude, but talking feels like this huge effort, like I don’t have the energy or the spark in me. Even when a client is being nice, I don’t really feel that warm feeling I used to get.\n\nAnd with friends, same thing. I used to go hiking with them and actually enjoy being outside and laughing and taking pictures and all that. Now even if they invite me, I mostly avoid it. It just sounds exhausting, and I don’t feel excited about it the way I should. It’s not that I hate everyone or anything… it’s more like the part of me that used to enjoy people and doing things is kind of muted.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your depressive symptoms are severe most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “severe” all the time, because some days I can still show up and sort of get through things, but… it feels serious, if that makes sense. Like it’s not just me being a little down or having a bad week.\n\nThe biggest thing is how much it’s changed how I function. At the salon, I used to be pretty reliable. My manager could count on me, clients trusted me, and I knew my routines. Now I’m making mistakes with basic stuff I’ve done over and over, or I’m working so slowly that everything gets backed up. And I can tell people notice. My manager seems like she doesn’t trust me the same way, and some clients don’t seem as comfortable with me, which really hurts because that used to be the part of my life where I felt competent.\n\nI’ve also been canceling things I used to handle without making a big deal out of it. Like responsibilities at work, or things I said I’d do, and then I just don’t follow through. It makes me feel ashamed because I know it looks like I don’t care, but it’s more like I can’t get myself together enough.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve pulled away from my friends a lot. I don’t show up like I used to. I also stopped doing the volunteer haircuts at the senior center, which I used to be really consistent with. That was something I actually cared about, and now I just… don’t go.\n\nSo I guess I’d say moderate to maybe really disruptive, most of the time. Maybe not the worst it could possibly be, but definitely enough that it’s messing up my work, my relationships, and the things I used to be dependable for.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about harming yourself?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, kind of. I don’t have, like, a plan to hurt myself or anything like that. I’m not sitting there thinking of a specific way to do it. But I do get these thoughts that scare me a little, or at least make me feel ashamed to say out loud.\n\nIt’s more like… I’ll think it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Like if I could just not have to deal with everything, and nobody else had to deal with me either. Not exactly that I’m trying to do something to myself, but more that disappearing or not existing for a while feels like it would be a relief.\n\nAnd then I feel guilty because I know that sounds awful, and I don’t want people to think I’m being dramatic. It’s just when I’m really low, my mind goes there, like everyone would be less burdened if I wasn’t around. But no, I don’t have a plan.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… not really. I mean, that’s part of what scares me to answer, because I don’t want it to sound like there’s some whole plan in my head. There isn’t. I haven’t sat down and thought, “This is how I would do it,” or picked anything out like that.
